ParlaMint-DK_20160512100908	The debate has been opened, and it is first the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Integration and Housing. Then I think we are getting ready to hear what the Minister is saying. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512101019	Thank you. The government is very concerned that we in Denmark should be able to attract skilled workers. We need a two-legged immigration policy. Denmark must have a strict and consistent foreign policy towards refugees and migrants, but at the same time we must be open to foreigners who can and will work and who will contribute to Danish society. Thus, more can help strengthen and develop Denmark. Danish companies must be able to cope with international competition. This requires companies to be able to recruit highly qualified and skilled employees. The government will therefore provide Danish companies with easier access to the foreign labour force. The arrangements for foreign labour must be targeted at the real needs of the labour market. The main road to Denmark must be through work and education, not through family reunification and asylum. In their draft legislation, the proposers refer to social dumping, but where is the actual evidence that the amount scheme is used for social dumping? This isn't about social dumping. Everyone agrees that foreigners as well as Danish citizens must work on normal pay and working conditions. It is therefore also a requirement to obtain a residence permit under the system of amounts that recruitment takes place under normal pay and employment conditions. We must not forget that DKK 375,000. is a minimum amount. Much of the wages are well above the DKK 375,000. Instead of talking about social dumping, we should have a political debate on who we want to give access to Denmark to work. I recognise that there may be a need to change the arrangements for foreign labour. The government wants to look at the real needs of the labour market and it may be appropriate to look at whether it is time to change the amount system as well. After all, the Left itself has proposed this. But it has to be done in context. What is crucial is that overall, the changes make Denmark more open to the foreign labour force that Danish companies demand. A lot can be said about timing, and I have heard the critical voices who believe that the government should have acted sooner in this area. To this I have to say that there have been other parts of the area of foreigners for which the government has spent a great deal of time, and I think many people understand that too. In addition, the government simply does not want the same thing in this area as the proposers do. The proposers want individual austerity that makes it harder for Danish companies to get the necessary manpower. The government wants to see the arrangements for foreign labour as a whole, so we ensure that companies have access to the labour force they need. If the companies do not, we risk moving from Denmark with the loss of Danish jobs as a result. I am therefore very sorry that it is apparently more important for the proposers that the government should come into the minority than to help Danish business to gain access to the necessary labour force. For it is therefore not an aid to Danish companies to carry out individual tightening of schemes for foreign labour. I cannot help wondering why the Socialists are taking part in this bill. It is only two years since a Social Democratic Minister for Employment carried out a reform of the area where the aim was for companies to have easier access to highly qualified labour; more to come here. At that time, the Socialists would not change the amount regime. Why, then, do the Socialists want to change the system now that companies are reporting labour shortages and unemployment is falling? I also need to ask the proposers how they intend to deal with cases pending when the law enters into force. That's not what the bill says. What amounts are to apply? In addition, it might also be nice to know which date the law should enter into force. Why should it be so difficult for companies and applicants to understand this? The government will later come up with a proposal for changes to the schemes for foreign labour. It will be an initiative that takes into account the real needs of companies for labour. This is an area which is very important and which, of course, also gives rise to a great deal of political debate. I hope that the parties of the Folketing will then be willing to engage in constructive dialogue, because the most important thing for the government is to ensure that the arrangements for foreign labour are targeted at the real needs of the labour market. So this is not ensured by such individual tightenings. That is why the government is going to vote against this proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512113030	The Green Card system should be abolished because it is counterproductive. That's how short it can really be said. Examples such as the newspaper Politiken has written about, with the 39-year-old man from Bangladesh, who came to Denmark in 2014 with a university degree in luggage, and who gave himself as a messenger who brought lunch dishes to Copenhagen, speak his own clear language. Denmark is at a time when many Danes and, above all, refugees are out of work in our country. It is therefore clear that it does not make much sense to attract highly qualified people from India or Bangladesh to do unskilled work and thus fill the jobs that could have gone to Danes or refugees. There must still be good opportunities to attract qualified labour to our country where we lack it, and fortunately there are too. The Group Scheme, the Amount Scheme and the Trusted List Scheme provide companies with the opportunity to get the workforce they need. Unfortunately, the government will not support this proposal. In the context of the proposal that we have discussed earlier today to raise the threshold of how much money should be earned annually in order to be able to be here on the money regime, there is unfortunately a picture of a government that is extremely slow to open our borders wide open to foreign labour, with wage pressure resulting. Considering how little the government does to combat social dumping in general, it may not be surprising, but nevertheless shameful. In Denmark we have an organised labour market. It is already under pressure from Eastern European employees who in too many cases have worked under conditions that are not in order. Why on earth should we voluntarily make that problem even bigger by also inviting additional labour from, for example. Asia for unskilled jobs? It doesn't make any sense unless you actually want to push the Danish salaries. We don't. For us, therefore, the matter is easy: we support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512114730	Thank you. We know that the President can tell the difference between us, so that's fine. It is about derogating from a fundamental principle which is enshrined in the law on aliens. The Green card system is that you do not need a separate work permit to access the Danish labour market. That is the fundamental difference. The basic principle of the Aliens Act is that in order to be able to work in this country as a foreigner, you must be able to show a contract of employment. You gotta have a job on your hand. The contract of employment shall indicate that the work is carried out under normal pay and working conditions. Unfortunately, it does not say that it should correspond to collective pay. It just says customary, and it's open to interpretation. Then the law on foreigners is also based on the fact that the social partners must be consulted locally, whether there is now a need for that labour. Now, these are quite sensible principles that have been introduced in the law on foreigners at the time. Unfortunately, it has been undermined by EU membership, which means that in reality the very simple and easy-to-understand rule does not apply when it comes to EU citizens and citizens from EEA countries. They are free to look for work and to take up work in this country without there being any requirement at what level of pay it is and whether the labour force is really needed. ParlaMint-DK_20160512131714	Thank you. A greencard scheme was introduced in 2007. In February 2008, the government then concluded an agreement with a number of parties on a job plan, including: the Greencard scheme was extended. The agreement on a job plan was intended to provide more labour in Denmark. The current economic recovery had led to historically high employment and the lowest measured unemployment since 1974. The recovery brought current pressures on the labour market, and many industries reported a shortage of labour. The original purpose of the greencard scheme was to attract qualified foreign labour. The scheme gives foreigners who can collect a certain number of points access to Denmark if they meet a number of specified requirements. There is the view of the Danish People's Party and I can also hear other parties' view that the greencard scheme has failed, as the scheme has not fulfilled the desired objective of ensuring Denmark qualified foreign workers working in its field of education. In addition, Denmark today faces quite different challenges than in 2008, when there was economic recovery and historically high employment. In addition, it must be considered inappropriate to have a greencard scheme which does not require a job agreement before entry. In addition, there are examples of social dumping and wage dumping in connection with the use of the green card system, and it is against this background that it is proposed that the greencard system be abolished. We regard this as a fair and balanced tightening up of the current rules on foreign labour, and I would like, in relation to those parties that have said that they support the bill, to acknowledge many times for their support. Then there have also been a number of criticisms regarding the process and the way in which the draft law is drafted. As regards the criticism of the date of entry into force, I think it is rather unmanageable, given that the government itself uses the same method of progress when the government makes legislative proposals. As far as I remember, this has been done most recently in relation to the rules on permanent residence permits, and it has also been done in the area of taxation. So it's a perfectly normal method of progress and practice that we're actually transferring to this bill. Then you can always discuss whether it is a reasonable practice. In the Danish People's Party we think it is a very reasonable practice, but at least it is a practice that has been seen in many other legislative proposals here in the Danish Parliament. ParlaMint-DK_20160512132321	The debate is open. And I give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Integration and Housing.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512132337	There should be no doubt that we in the government fully agree with the proposers in their desire to limit the influx of asylum seekers to Denmark. The government is therefore also pursuing a consistent immigration policy to ensure an open Denmark for those who can and will and close the doors to those who will not. As you know, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Conservative People's Party and the Left have introduced an integration benefit that makes it less attractive to come to Denmark and makes it more attractive to become self-supporting, that is to say to enter the labour market, get a job and learn the Danish language. In addition, with the same parties plus the Socialists, we have made a number of other significant changes in the area of foreigners. Firstly, in November 2015, we adopted asylum package I, which provides the authorities with a number of new tools to deal with an increase in the number of refugees and migrants who enter and stay in Denmark and ensure the maintenance of order. Secondly, in December, we adopted legislation allowing for the possibility of implementing transporter responsibility when travelling to Denmark from another Schengen country, if Denmark introduces temporary border controls. Thirdly, in January of this year we had asylum package II implemented, which contains some very significant tightenings in the area of asylum and migration in order to make it significantly less attractive to apply to Denmark. I believe that these austerity measures must have an equal impact on the number of foreigners who apply to Denmark. Of course, the number is also related to the situation in the world around us. And even though the number of people entering Denmark has fallen since the government introduced border controls on Germany on 4 January, the pressure on the EU's external borders remains high. In addition, the number of asylum seekers in Europe is still historically high. It is against this background that the Government informed the European Commission on 29 April that the Government has extended temporary border controls until 2 June. It is against this background that the government, together with the governments of Sweden, Germany, Austria and France, has asked the Commission to help extend border controls for up to two years. I can also see that we have been extended by 1⁄2 years here today. With the proposal we are debating here today, the proposers will reject asylum seekers at the Danish border. This may sound like an easy and very enticing solution to the refugee and migration crisis that we are facing, but it is not quite that simple. It follows from the Dublin Regulation that asylum applications can be lodged at our borders. The Dublin Regulation also lays down detailed rules on which European country is responsible for examining an application for asylum. Thus, if an alien seeks asylum at our borders, it must always be assessed whether he or she can be transferred to another Dublin country or whether he or she should have his or her asylum application examined here in Denmark. The principles of the Dublin rules are sensible, and the aim is precisely to ensure that asylum seekers cannot shop around the EU countries. Having said that, it is no secret that the Dublin system is currently being challenged. The government is, of course, following the situation very closely. The way forward is therefore, in my view, to work hard at first to ensure that the Dublin system works so that we can prevent asylum seekers from freely choosing which country they are seeking asylum in. It is also worth noting in this context that a proposal has recently been put forward for reform of the Dublin system and that of course Denmark is doing what we can to ensure that the Dublin system remains on track. Following the motion for a resolution, the authors of the motion will not only reject asylum seekers at the border but also want them to be able to do so after one hour's proceedings. To that I must say that it is not realistic to carry out the entire asylum procedure in just one hour at the border. We have an obligation to carry out an objective and thorough asylum procedure, which means that the immigration authorities must assess each individual asylum application thoroughly, and so it often takes more than one hour to deal with an asylum case today. In addition, it is purely practical, which I also think is important for the proposers that asylum seekers have the right to stay in the country, while a possible refusal of asylum is dealt with in the Refugee Board. And if people were refused after only one hour's treatment, it would be possible to imagine that relatively many people would be able to complain about their asylum case to the Refugee Board, and so we have an obligation to house asylum seekers while the matter is settled there. There are therefore unfortunately no snapshot solutions to the extraordinary situation in which Denmark, Scandinavia and the rest of Europe find themselves. In my opinion, however, we need to get the EU's external borders under control in the first place. This will help to bring fewer asylum seekers to Denmark. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution as it stands. But, of course, I look forward very much to the further consideration of the resolution here in the Folketing and look forward to the debate it may create.

We are aware that there are different interpretations of, for example, the Dublin Regulation, but we believe that Denmark should explore the possibilities of rejection at the Danish borders within the conventions. I also believe that the national austerity of other countries means something to the influx to Denmark, too, that other nation states have introduced more and more border controls. The fact that there has been a slowing down, I believe, is largely due to the fact that many countries have implemented austerity measures and cannot be attributed to the fact that the European Union has taken various steps. One major reason why we are dealing with these problems is, in fact, that the common EU rules that are in this area have actually collapsed. So, from our perspective, the European Union is not part of the solution, but is more part of the problem. One might also hope that if Denmark starts to reject asylum seekers at the border, other European countries will do the same, which will effectively limit the flow. It is to be expected, moreover, that if the resolution is implemented, there will be considerable savings in public budgets as the number of asylum seekers is expected to decrease significantly. This proposal will ensure a better protection against a new violent rhyme in Denmark, and it will allow us to once again gain a foothold and stand firm on our culture and our traditions. That is what it is all about, at least in the case of the Danish People's Party. And, as I said, this will lead to long-term improvements in the Danish economy. So there are all sorts of good arguments for voting in favour, but it is the case with good arguments in this House that they need to mature for a while.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512143016	First of all, as I have done a number of times, I would like to make it clear that for me and for the government, getting Danish citizenship is something very special. That is why I am also incredibly pleased that in the autumn a broad majority in the Folketing helped to change the guidelines for citizenship. In this context, let me stress that I think it has been a very great strength, that we have a broad majority and that we could implement this tightening up broadly, and also that it was across the middle that it happened. For it is something special to become a Danish citizen, and it is very good that it stretches right across the middle of the Assembly Hall. With the agreement, the bar for when to call oneself Dane has been lifted, and it helps to stress that getting a Danish citizenship is something very special. It's something you have to earn. One of the practical results of the agreement is that the career periods for applicants who have committed criminal offences have been extended. We must impose strict criminal requirements on those who wish to become Danish citizens. When it comes to fines, it follows from the guidelines that fines of DKK 3,000 are imposed. and above prevent admission to a bill on the notification of citizenship for four years and six months from the date of the offence. However, fines for drunk driving and infringements of drug law are non-negotiable and will therefore trigger a waiting period, no matter what the amount of the fine is. As far as infringements of all laws other than criminal law, arms law and the law on drugs are concerned, the proposers want to raise the level of time for a fine to trigger Karens from the current DKK 3,000. 10,000 kroner. I am very pleased with the stricter rules on crime resulting from the current circular. The rules help to stress that being able to call oneself Dane is something special that I have mentioned, and we certainly see no reason to relax the requirements. It has been the case in the past that in some cases the application of the Karen rules has led to an inappropriate result. Many may recall the so - called weed burner. We have taken this into account in the agreement on citizenship of the autumn, when we have introduced a new extension option for the Committee on Bodily Rights. These are fines for infringements of legislation other than criminal law, drug law, arms law and traffic law at the level of DKK 3 000 to 10,000. in penalty. The Committee therefore has a specific opportunity to assess the circumstances of each case and to decide whether the matter should be dealt with in accordance with the rules of procedure or whether a derogation should be granted. The motion for a resolution is marked by the fact that the authors of the motion for a resolution particularly want to relax the rules on coaching in relation to fines for infringements of the Road Traffic Code. To this end, I would like to start by stressing that, as I said, the Road Traffic Code is expressly exempted from the new extension access. During the negotiations on the agreement, it was discussed in detail whether fines for infringements of the Road Traffic Code should be covered by possible extension access and, in the light of the discussions, it was agreed that there should be no exemption when it comes to infringements of the Road Traffic Code. I think there are many good reasons for this. Let me give you a few examples of when a violation of the Road Traffic Code results in a fine of between DKr 3 000 and 10,000. If you drive a car without taking your driver's license, you will be fined DKK 7,000 for the first time you have been stopped. If you are then stopped again, yes, it triggers a fine of DKK 8,500 as a starting point. According to the proposers, many penalties for speed violations are more than DKK 3,000. To this I would like to stress that if the speed violations in an urban zone are to trigger a fine of DKK 3 000, it requires that the driver has driven a car at least 70 km/h. at a point where the speed limit is 50 km/h and that is to say, at least 40% of the speed limit must be exceeded. If you find yourself on a motorway where the speed limit is 110 km/h, it will require that the driver of the car has driven at least 165 km/h before being punished with a fine of DKK 3,000. or above, which means that you must have driven at least 50 per cent. faster than allowed. Moreover, such a speed violation would result in a conditional driver's right to disqualification. On the other hand, minor speed violations will trigger fines of at least DKK 3,000. and therefore do not cause a karen's. I think it is reasonable that you cannot obtain Danish citizenship for a period of time if you have been driving without a driving licence or if you have been driving more than 40%. faster than allowed. Let me sum up quite briefly: the government cannot support the resolution because the government is satisfied with the very broad agreement on citizenship concluded last autumn. I think, by the way, that I said something quite wrong at some point, because I said that if you had not got your driving licence, it triggered a fine of DKK 7 000. That is, of course, not correct. It is if you have not taken a driving licence that it triggers a fine of DKK 7,000.

In general, until 2015, fines of DKK 3,000 were imposed. or above led to a waiting period. Then we got the so-called hedge burner case and I'm glad that Mr. Jan E. Jørgensen has explained what is a weed burner and what is a hedge burner where a man from Rask Mølle accidentally set his hedge on fire, and there it was so that if you had received a fine of more than DKK 3,000, you could not obtain citizenship. Now, the parties behind the 2015 birthright agreement have decided to make a referral scheme, which means that fines of between DKK 3,000. and 10,000 kroner. may be submitted to the Committee of Birth Rights for a derogation. I would like to congratulate the majority on this, because I think that is very sensible. In fact, I think that there are limits to what should mean that you can no longer acquire Danish citizenship. In fact, I think there is a small limit, and I have heard both the Left, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservatives say so in the past. When I think that is sensible, I would, of course, like to see this also apply to infringements of the traffic law, and I have again heard several parties here in the Folketing say so. It is simply not wise for the individual or for Denmark that we reject potential citizens because they have committed something that I would call a trivial offense. It's not because it's okay to drive too fast and it's okay to have a faulty belt. Of course it's not. They're being punished, too. But it's about whether or not we should punish these people twice. I don't think we should. I believe that there is a limit, and I hope that a favourable view will also be given to a system of presentation in the field of road traffic. I actually heard Mr. Jan E. Jørgensen says that it could be a quite excellent model and that there have still been cases where Mr. Jan E. Jørgensen thought it was a little crazy that you couldn't get citizenship, for example. in the case of Mohamad and the worn-out tires and a defective harness. ParlaMint-DK_20160512154901	Thank you for that and thank you for the debate. Last year, a majority in the Folketing introduced a new scheme so that applicants who are fined between DKr 3,000 and 10,000. Under all laws other than the Criminal Code, the Road Traffic Act, the Law on Arms and the Law on Drugs, their cases are referred to the Committee on the Rights of the Child with the possibility of a derogation. And that change was made, as we know, in connection with the case of the gradually known hedge burner in Denmark, an older man who accidentally set the neighbor's hedge on fire because he removed weeds with a weed burner out on the sidewalk. The problem is that the vast majority of fines we have been asked about in recent years are not about all these other laws, but exactly about the traffic law. The 3,000 kroner. is a de minimis limit, and it is fixed at a time when traffic tickets were at a lower level than they are today. 3,000 kroner. is today the fixed rate if you talk in handheld mobile phone at the same time and forget the belt. 3,000 kroner. If you forget your driver's license at home in the drawer and drive with a child without a child's chair, it costs. If you overtake a bar and forget to blink, the price is also DKK 3,000. In a large number of speed overrun cases, the fines amount to DKK 3,000. Is it okay to break traffic laws? No, of course not. Of course, infringements of the traffic law must be punished, and that is what we have traffic penalties for. The only question is whether some road users should be penalised twice, i.e. both with a fine and with a quarantine from obtaining citizenship. All the parties in here have agreed to the principle that there should be a de minimis limit, and today it is 3,000 DKK. In other words, what we are discussing is not whether it is the case that if we ever do the least with regard to traffic law, it means no to citizenship and a career period. No, there is a recognition that there must be a trivial limit. So the question is, should it be 3,000 kroner?? Sir. Jan E. ParlaMint-DK_20160512160203	Thank you for the floor, President, and also thank you for having this debate. This year's energy policy report describes another exciting year for Danish energy policy. What I am particularly aware of when I read the report is that international developments are increasingly helping to set the direction for the Danish energy policy. This applies both globally, regionally and within the framework of the European Union. Globally, in 2015, it was specifically the conclusion of the Paris Global Climate Agreement at COP21 that will set a track. The Paris Agreement is a turning point in global climate action, because all countries are now involved in a commitment to a global transition to low-mission, and the transition we have long had in this country has thus been given a global hanging, which we have not seen before. The Danish objective of independence from fossil fuels was established under the government of Anders Fogh Rasmussen III and is in that sense prior to its time. It is in line with the new global objective that greenhouse gas emissions should peak as soon as possible and then be reduced quickly. The fact is that we in this country have long been pursuing an ambitious energy policy with very renewable energy in the system, high security of supply and focus on the development of green energy technology. That is, in principle, the course that many other countries now have to take. With the clear international direction, it is even more obvious that Denmark should not pursue its own isolated energy policy, but should take into account that we are connected with the rest of the world, both concretely, politically and economically. Regional cooperation and interconnection offer many benefits in terms of increased security of supply and lower costs. It is therefore important that we continue and develop cooperation on energy with our neighbouring countries. Denmark must play an active role and press for a continued ambitious energy policy. Danish energy supply and energy policy are also increasingly influenced by the European agenda, but it is only good because it offers better opportunities for more efficient use of a Danish energy system and ensures continued security of supply at less cost than if Denmark were alone. It is the Government's vision that energy should flow freely in Europe and that an internal energy market should be created through the Energy Union. A more integrated energy market and more coherent energy systems regionally and in the EU will bring positive benefits for security of supply, trade opportunities and the transition to renewable energy. There is no doubt that the European framework for our energy policy in the coming years will have an impact on the way in which we organise future support for, among other things. renewable energy. I am confident that the challenges will be solved and that we will reap the many benefits of greater integration of energy policy across the EU Member States. But not only must we let European development come to us, but we must also help to create it ourselves. In this context, there may be a need to think new and creatively, precisely because the framework is clearly changing. This is one of the reasons why the government has now set up an Energy Commission which held its first meeting on Monday, 9 May. The Energy Commission, consisting of nine members from the research world and industry, will analyse and assess developments in the energy sector in a regional and European context and make recommendations for Danish energy policy for the period 2020-2030. The Energy Commission will work throughout 2016 and is expected to be completed with a comprehensive report and recommendations at the beginning of 2017. I look forward very much to what the Energy Commission has to contribute and how it can promote the government's objective of the green transition in a cost-effective way. It is an essential element for a cost-effective transformation of the Danish energy sector that conditions for Danish companies need to be improved. One of the things that has filled a great deal in the past year is the concern about industrial energy prices and competitiveness. The PRS tax has been increasing in recent years due to falling energy prices and increasing renewable energy expansion. This, as well as the fact that the European Commission has criticised the Danish PSO system, means that in the course of 2016 we will have to find a long-term PSP solution, and the government is very concerned about this. The same goes for the follow-up to the energy agreement and its costs. In other areas too, we need to look at the economy. In the 2014 Growth Agreement, it was agreed that efficiency in the supply sectors would be increased to a total of 3.3 billion. kr. in 2020. This means that the utilities must deliver electricity, gas and district heating and water and handle waste and waste water more efficiently than today. This will benefit both households and the competitiveness of energy and water-using companies and thus Danish jobs. In 2016, therefore, the Government is presenting a strategy for the future supply sectors, including: will describe the efficiency potential of the sectors after 2020. In the gas sector too, there is potential for a more efficient and competitive sector. An inter-ministerial working group has analysed the possibilities in a report, and this has resulted in a number of recommendations on how the gas sector can be made more efficient. In the area of district heating, on 7 April this year, the Government concluded an agreement on a new regulation of the district heating sector. The new regulation must modernise the principle of self-reliance by excluding each company from a cost framework that is continuously reduced. Finally, in the electricity sector too, efficiency improvements need to be made. Based on its 64 recommendations for a new regulation of the electricity sector, the government has taken a general view in September 2015 on the actions it wishes to take. The measures leading to legislative changes are incorporated into a new Electricity Act, which is expected to enter into force on 1 January 2018. Another area that is also changing is the use of Danish resources for energy production. Since 1995, Denmark has been a net exporter of oil and gas, a position which Denmark is planning to maintain to at least 2021 for oil and 2023 for natural gas with current forecasts. Danish oil and gas production is of great importance for the Danish economy and not least for the state's income. There is still considerable potential for further production, but the challenges in the oil sector are many and large, not least the relatively low oil price and increased international competition. The government, together with the industry, is therefore preparing an oil and gas strategy for the future exploration and commercial use of Danish resources. The good news is that the seventh round of invitations to tender has shown that, regardless of the challenges, there is still a great interest in the companies to investigate in the Danish part of the North Sea. The Danish Energy Agency received more applications than in the previous rounds, and no fewer than 16 licenses were granted to 12 different companies. As you know, there are other energy installations at sea than drilling platforms, namely offshore wind farms. The offer of Horns Rev 3 was concluded with a winning settlement price that is significantly below the level of aid in the other countries with offshore wind turbines. Outside Denmark wind turbines on land are the renewable energy technology, which excuses the term "have the most wind in the sails," simply because it is often the most efficient and the cheapest way to establish new production capacity. Denmark and Danish companies have an important role to play in this development, with decades of experience and industrial world class in the field, and we should be able to continue to deliver solutions to the world. The public support for research, development and demonstration of new environmentally friendly and cost-effective energy technologies is expected to amount to around 790 million in 2016. DKK, including an expected EU-residence of around 345 million. kr. That is a great deal of money in a time that calls for sharp priorities across the political objectives. Denmark also participates with a number of other countries in the US initiative Mission Innovation, which was launched on the opening day of COP 21 in Paris. Based on our participation in Mission Innovation, the government will work to ensure that in future we significantly strengthen research in the field of clean energy technologies. In conclusion, I would like to emphasise that broad consensus among the parliamentary parties on energy policy is essential if we are to ensure a cost-effective transition that is supported by both the population and industry. Investment in the energy sector is often very long-term and the stable framework that the broad energy reconciliations provide is needed. The government will unceasingly work to ensure that Denmark also has an ambitious energy policy in the future with broad political support, and I look forward to continuing constructive dialogue on how best to achieve the energy policy objectives for Denmark. Thank you for the floor.

Therefore, one must be very careful to create uncertainty, say in the media, that one is considering scraping elements of an energy reconciliation that is actually widely agreed in the Folketing. Then you can be tempted to say that it is a little cheap to say: After the next election we promise that a lot of money will be spent on energy research. Finally, I would like to say that we in the Social Democratic Party are a little critical of it, you might say, deviation from the 2050 target, which I think is more and more in government policy, where we are talking about this fossil independence, which is no longer to be understood as being completely free from fossil fuels, but which just has to be understood as meaning that if we build an extra offshore wind farm and send the flow out of the country, we can still use some of the other. I just think that we need to realise that what is going to happen abroad in the coming years is not how to produce more renewable energy, how to build more offshore wind farms... they will also be asking that for some time yet... but what they are going to ask about in 30 years is how to avoid having the coal power plant backup? That is why, of course, we also have to show in practice that we can run a society, even a prosperous society, without fossil fuels. That is why the objective that we must be free of fossil fuels by 2050, is important. It should not just be the case that, on average, we have to produce as much renewable energy as we use energy. It is a weakening of that ambition, and I think it will also be a weakening of Danish business. I shall conclude by saying that this is what is universally superior to social democracy, namely that while we are doing this, because we think we have a responsibility towards future generations and the climate of the planet, we are doing it because in Denmark we actually have a position of strength in this area and because it is one of the things that can create jobs, and not just jobs for the longest-trained people in the large cities, but also jobs for the 3Fs, for the metal workers around those parts of the country where you most need new jobs. ParlaMint-DK_20160512164803	Thank you for that, President. I am pleased that today we have occasion to debate and focus on Denmark's energy policy. The Government has presented an energy policy statement for 2016, and I welcome what has emerged in the energy policy statement. The statement shows that Denmark has a strong position in finding good energy solutions. We are a pioneering country in the green area. At the same time, the report shows that there is room for improvement, because when we then try to maintain our green leadership, we must also be aware that the green transition must not cause unnecessarily high costs for Danish industry and the Danish taxpayers. We want to support energy policy to promote green conversion in a cost-effective way that contributes to growth and employment. It is not enough for us to invest massively in green energy solutions. We must also ensure that we are competitive, otherwise growth and jobs will move to countries with less restrictive energy requirements. One of the points highlighted in the energy statement, which I would also like to highlight here, is that Denmark should not pursue its own isolated energy policy. This leads nowhere, because the Danish energy system is closely linked to the rest of the world, and if we are to increase security of supply and lower costs, it requires cooperation across national borders. Nor can Denmark solve the world's energy problems alone. Neither are we. Instead, Denmark must work to put pressure on other countries to pursue an ambitious energy policy. It is global energy accounting that ultimately counts. Energy efficiency is a key part of the energy policy statement. In Denmark, we are good at finding smart energy solutions. We're famous for that in the world. We have also succeeded in increasing our exports of energy technology. In 2014, energy technology was no less than 12%. of the total Danish exports of goods. It's something we can be proud of. Analyses have shown that the energy intensity of Danish companies is among the lowest in the EU. This is partly due to the fact that we are so good at energy efficiency and our work on energy efficiency continues. Danish energy policy must help to strengthen our competitiveness. It does this by maintaining high ambitions and focusing on green realism, and that is precisely the policy of the Left. Left is working to connect Denmark closer to abroad. We work to ensure good energy solutions for as little money as possible. With these words, the Left fully endorses the conclusions and analyses contained in the statement. We look forward to the continued work in producing a good, sensible energy policy for Denmark, an energy policy that helps to solve the energy challenges, while ensuring growth, development and jobs in Denmark.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512181251	The debate is open, and that is the Minister for the Environment and Food. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512181308	Thank you for that, President, and thank you to the Enhedslisten for tabling motion for a resolution B 150. The proposed test of women's urine should provide a basis for advice on, among other things: diet for women who want to become pregnant and women who are already pregnant. I fully agree with the proposers that unborn children should not be harmed by chemical substances from the environment or food, and this also applies to pesticide residues. It is important that, at the level of society, we protect future generations from the undesirable effects of harmful substances, and this is done through research, setting safe limit values and general advice to the population, including pregnant women. Let me therefore first of all state that it is an important objective of the government that food on the Danish market contains as few pesticide residues as possible. This also means that limit values for residues in food must be set at health-friendly levels. However, I do not believe that an offer to test the urine of each woman is necessary to ensure that pregnant women, and hence the unborn children, are not exposed to harmful levels of pesticide residues. We already have a system of setting maximum levels for pesticide residues in food to ensure that we are not exposed to pesticide residues in harmful amounts. When a limit value for pesticide residues in food is set in the EU, there is a comprehensive risk assessment behind which includes all relevant knowledge of the substance available. The latest knowledge in this field is always taken into account when setting maximum levels for pesticide residues and therefore the limit values are constantly revised in relation to new knowledge about the substances. Food control by the Danish Food Authority shows that the maximum levels for pesticide residues are generally observed in both Danish and foreign fruit and vegetables. The results of the Danish Food Authority's random checks are collected every five years in a major monitoring report where the Danes intake of pesticide residues from the diet is assessed. The latest monitoring report from 2013 shows, as before, that the total intake of pesticide residues by Danes is well below the level where there would be health concerns, and the monitoring report even takes into account combination effects because the diet contains residues of many different pesticides. In other words, consumers can feel quite safe about the food they buy, even if they contain residues of pesticides, and so it must be. The controls also show that Danish fruit and vegetables contain fewer pesticide residues and have a much lower pesticide content than foreign fruit and vegetables. Studies have actually shown that the Danes can halve their pesticide intake just by eating Danish fruit and vegetables instead of foreign fruit and vegetables, and that is a good argument for buying Danish. On its website, the Danish Food Safety Authority provides general advice to consumers and special advice targeted pregnant women on pesticide residues in food. Guides on the website tell how consumers can reduce their intake of pesticide residues, among others. by choosing organic or Danish crops where possible. The question may be asked: What will a pregnant woman be able to use information about the levels of pesticide residues in the urine for? The fact that pesticide residues can be detected in the urine does not mean that it is dangerous. It is actually expected that residues can be detected in the urine as the body disposes of pesticides and their degradation products along that path. When you eat food with a legal and safe content of pesticide residues, residues of pesticides and degradation products can also be found in the urine. Nor does the contents of the urine say anything about the source. Although the assessment is that food content is the main source of residues in the urine, the residues can also come from e.g. the working environment or the use of pesticides against insects indoors in buildings. A test would not only be unnecessary in providing dietary advice to pregnant women, but would also impose financial burdens on society, among others. and individual dietary advice and, at the same time, would increase the concern unnecessarily in pregnant women. We must be careful that fruit and vegetables do not get a reputation as dangerous. If you avoid fruit and vegetables because you are afraid of pesticides, there is a risk that less healthy foods will be chosen as substitutes. The recommendation to eat at least 600 grams of fruit and vegetables a day continues to apply, even if it may contain pesticide residues in quantities which are not harmful to health. A residual content, which is safe from health and as low as possible, is an essential point of reference in the current pesticide strategy, which is based on a broad political settlement. The government is working for safe food, including that pesticide residues in food are at as low a level as possible. A test of the urine of pregnant women for pesticide residues will not contribute to the pregnant woman's ability to act on a professionally based basis. I therefore believe that an offer to test the urine of each woman is not the right way to go if it is to be ensured that pregnant women are not exposed to harmful levels of pesticide residues. This is also not necessary in order to provide dietary advice to pregnant women, and therefore the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512182546	In the Social Democratic Party we are very concerned about the health of Danish women, both those who are pregnant and those who want to be. That is why, of course, we also find it worrying that Danish consumers are today surrounded by food that can contain pesticide residues if it can be the real cause of brain damage in children. It is important for us that pregnant women receive good and useful advice as part of their pregnancy in order to prevent possible harm to the foetuses. Equally important is the fact that women who intend to become pregnant are aware of the risks that may be related to fertility. In Denmark fertility has been challenged for many years, and more and more children come into the world using fertility treatment. Doctors believe that this is not due to anything genetic, but is due to lifestyle or endocrine disruptors. Therefore, the influence of harmful substances on fertility must be taken very seriously. That is also why, when we were in government, the Socialists set aside 50 million. kr. in the Finance Act for a newly created research centre at the Righospital. The Centre should be conducting research into the substances that scientists suspect to harm people's ability to have children. The centre was supposed to gather and create new knowledge and thus be one of the leading centres in the world in the area. The present government has subsequently chosen to cut support for the Centre before it has even started, and that is a huge deterioration. In the long term, research into endocrine disruptors should have helped Danes with fertility problems, and the intention of setting up a research centre to research hormone disruptors was to ensure new knowledge in the field. This knowledge could have qualified the advice and guidance given to pregnant women today in connection with their pregnancy. We believe that prevention is the right way to go, and we also think it is appropriate to look at how prevention efforts for pregnant women and women and men intending to become pregnant could be targeted and improved even more. However, we cannot support the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution to introduce a free test for pesticide residues for all women who intend to become or have become pregnant. It is above all because Enhedslisten does not indicate how it is intended to finance the proposal. Nor do we believe that, on this basis, it is possible to decide whether a free test of pesticide residues is the right place to take action or whether action should be focused elsewhere. For how should you react to such a test as a pregnant woman somewhere inside your pregnancy? Maybe you're not so far along that it's too late to have an abortion. But is this the decision to be made? Is it not a risk that a woman who gets a test that shows a certain level of pesticide residues will regret her pregnancy and become concerned perhaps also unnecessarily? Moreover, are the authors of the motion not also vulnerable to double gender discrimination in this area? Because why don't men have to be tested before you plan to get pregnant? Why should women bear all the burden and responsibility alone? We think that this is a matter that the proposal does not relate to and, overall, we have made up our minds that we cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512184119	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Erling Bonnesen, Left, go ahead.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512184133	Thank you. The Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution involves the introduction of a free offer to test the levels of pesticide residues in urine for all women who intend to become or are pregnant. The test will be the basis for dietary advice so that women can reduce the intake of pesticides, which could possibly harm the child. The Left agrees with the Enhedslisten that pesticide residues should be reduced as much as possible. For future generations, it is important that we protect unborn children from chemical substances from the environment and food. Offering individual testing of urine for pesticide residues, however, is not the solution. Firstly, food on the Danish market is generally free from pesticide residues in harmful amounts, and the fixing of limit values for pesticide residues in food takes into account all population groups. This also applies to pregnant women. Secondly, the fact that pesticide residues can be found in the urine is not the same as that it is harmful. It is generally to be expected that residues of pesticides will be found in human urine. The body disposes of pesticides and their degradation products through the urine. Thirdly, pesticide residues in the urine come not only from the diet but also from other external sources, such as from the use of biocides against insects in the home, i.e. insect spray, or when exposed to the substances in the working environment. Therefore, if pesticide residues are found in the urine, it is not necessarily because of the diet. Therefore, it is typically food such as fruit and vegetables that is shown more in foreign than domestic food containing pesticide residues. Fruit is an important part of a varied diet. It would not be advantageous to scare pregnant women away from fruit and vegetables because pesticide residues can be detected in the urine. That is why the Left does not believe that an offer of free testing for pesticide residues as part of dietary advice to pregnant women is the right way to ensure future generations against the damage caused by pesticide residues, and that is why the Left cannot support the motion for a resolution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512192815	The debate is open. We shall first give the floor to the Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512192836	Thank you for that, President. Thank you to the Enhedslisten for tabling motion for a resolution B 153. The proposal provides for the lifting of the ban on certain breeds of dogs and for the introduction of mandatory measures focusing on ownership responsibility, including the possibility of a requirement to participate in behavioural courses, the requirement to neutralize the dog and the possibility of banning the wearing of dogs in cases where the dog has shown aggressive behaviour. The 2010 amendment of the Dog Law introduced a ban on 13 breeds of dogs, two of which were already prohibited. The ban was imposed on the basis of a series of serious incidents in which people or animals were attacked by dangerous dogs. It created an increased fear among the population of being attacked by dogs on public streets and roads. The ban on certain breeds was then introduced with the expectation that the number of dangerous dogs could be reduced gradually, while at the same time safeguarding the safety and safety of citizens. The Enhedslisten tabled a similar motion for a resolution back in 2013. At that time, an initiative had already been taken to evaluate, among other things, The ban on dogs. The ban scheme was evaluated by the University of Copenhagen in 2013 and, on the basis of the evaluation, it was found that the ban scheme had the desired effect on the number of banned dogs; that there is indication that the ban scheme has had a positive effect on the citizens' experience of safety and security; that it is not possible to de- or confirm whether the ban scheme of the dog law has had a reducing effect on episodes where people have been bitten by dogs; and that it suggests that the ban has had some effect on bite episodes involving other dogs. As a whole, the Ministry of Food did not consider it appropriate to propose a change to the prohibition scheme and the amendment to the Dog Law in 2014 did not therefore concern the ban regime of the Dog Law. The dog law already provides an opportunity for the police to issue orders, among other things. whether a dog should be kept on a short leash and/or wear a muzzle. In connection with the amendment of the Dog Law in 2010, the Ministry of Justice set up a working group which, among other things: considered the possibility of different alternatives to a prohibition scheme, including the possibility of requiring to take part in the course. The Committee had, inter alia: the opinion that it would not be appropriate to introduce the possibility for the police to require a dog owner to participate in a course if his dog has actually caused damage or is at risk to his environment. This is justified by the fact that, in order to benefit from a course, it will require the dog owner to participate constructively in the training course, and there is a significant risk that this will not be the case if participation in the course takes place on instructions from the police. The question of how best to ensure the safety and safety of citizens in relation to the interests of dog owners is complex. Prior to the introduction of the ban scheme, the debate focused on the fear of the dangerous dogs and dog bites. Today, the media are focusing on the opposite: the debate on whether or not we went too far when the provision on banned breeds of dogs was introduced. Although the debate now focuses on whether or not we went too far against a ban system, we must not forget that it was fear and uncertainty among the population that were the basis for the introduction of a ban system. I think it is important that the dog law takes into account both the safety and safety of the citizens and the interests of the dog owners and the welfare of the dogs. As I said, it is a quite complex balancing exercise. It is my view that, with the recent amendment to the Dog Law in 2014, where we, among other things, are involved. was given the opportunity to include the circumstances of the bite episode in the evaluation of the stool bite, created a better balance. I do not believe that, with the abolition of the ban system and the introduction of additional conditions, we can effectively take account of the citizens' experience of security and security and, in this context, the government cannot vote in favour of the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512194819	Thank you. Since, on this proposal, the rapporteur of the Left is present in the Chamber but has taken the chair, I have promised to read the speech. The Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution means that the ban on certain breeds of dogs is lifted and that ownership of the dog's behaviour is instead introduced. When the ban on dogs was introduced in 2010, it was due to a series of serious incidents both on humans and animals that had been attacked by dangerous dogs. This caused fear of being attacked on the street, and therefore the ban system was introduced with an expectation of more security and security in the population. A total of 13 breeds of dogs were banned with the ban scheme. The prohibition scheme was evaluated in 2013. The evaluation showed that there had been a decrease in the number of breeds included in the prohibition list and that there was an indication that the prohibition scheme has had a positive effect on the citizens' experience of safety and security. In other words, the prohibition system has fulfilled its purpose. Since 2013, no new information has been available justifying a re-evaluation or giving rise to a change in the prohibition regime. In addition, the dog law as it stands today contains the possibility of injunctions. This means that the police can issue orders that dogs, for example, should wear a muzzle. The best way to ensure the safety and safety of our citizens at the same time as regards the dog owner and the welfare of the dog is a difficult balance. However, we must not forget in the heat of the debate that the fear of attacking dangerous dogs was the reason why the ban system was introduced at all. In the absence of any new information to change the ban regime, and in view of the fact that we cannot effectively guarantee the safety and security of our citizens without the ban regime, the Left is voting against the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512201648	Thank you very much. When we propose to abolish this racial ban, it is primarily because we have been able to see that there has been no effect of this racial ban, that is, that there are no fewer people and dogs that have been bitten since it was introduced. I think that is very, very important to look at, namely that this law has obviously not had the desired effect. And then there is good reason to look at having it changed. The second is that this legislation has also led to a very, very unfortunate situation for a great many dog owners who are responsible and who just want to be allowed to have their dog, and who have at the same time experienced that innocent dogs have been killed, that they are put into long-term quarantine and that they simply are not able to handle this law in a sensible way. It is also very challenging for people's legal certainty. So I think we have a really good opportunity here in the Folketing to change the dog law and remove this racial ban and instead to introduce ownership responsibility for the dog's behavior, so that we can actually get rid of the problems that still exist with dog bites, because I think that is very, very important to ensure that the population and dog owners and small children etc. can be safe when we walk around other people's dogs. It can be said that this whole situation arises from the fact that in 2009 there was an enormous amount of publicity in the media about violent incidents of dog bites, and several rapporteurs have pointed out that we are not seeing the same media talk anymore. I just think, unfortunately, that this is not because there have been fewer incidents, but just because the focus has moved somewhere else. That is precisely why we would actually like to introduce something which actually worked and which is documented in many other countries. When Parliament adopted this ban on dog breeds in 2010, several experts and veterinarians warned against it and said that this was not the right way to go. But they chose to overhear that. And now I think that the examples and studies that have come to light since then support the fact that the effect is not there. I really think we should take this very, very seriously. It can be said that since this law was introduced it was introduced retroactively... thousands of dogs and puppies have been killed, and that is also true, it can be said, crossbreeds where these breeds of dogs are just included. So it's really a lot of dogs we're talking about. As I mentioned earlier, in 2013 the University of Copenhagen has carried out an evaluation of the effect of the racial ban and has thus been able to establish that the ban has not worked and that it is apparently not going to work either. They then also point out that there is the effect that there are some who feel more secure. However, in my view it is a false security when it is not based on real reductions in bites, and when it is something that has come at the expense of a great many other people, we can see that too, because a great many frustrated dog owners have written to us that has become deeply unhappy about this and that very many animals have suffered under this legislation. In addition to that, it can be said that there is also a huge problem in relation to the police's heavy workload in enforcing this racial ban. There are simply policemen who need to use resources to walk around and find out if you can spot whether or not some of the banned dogs are on the street. There are many cases where it does not succeed very well and the dogs are interned for a very, very long time, although they should not have been. That is one of the things I think we must take note of, so that there are the very unfortunate consequences of this. It has to be said that the result of all these resources that have been put into enforcing the race ban has apparently not had a very good effect, and left a lot of dog owners have a sense of being lawless and powerless, and they feel unfairly treated and incredibly frustrated, one might say, over the Danish legal system. I think that is something we should try to avoid in the world, namely that our legislation feels unfair and arbitrary for people in this way. It can be said that when the ban on these specific breeds was introduced, it was not based on a high level of objectivity in relation to what kind of races were chosen. I mean, basically, I just think that this has missed out, even when we look at international experience. There are simply a large number of countries that have introduced this and that have later rolled the race ban back because they have seen that it has not worked. And so, for example, you have... in Canada had some really, really good experiences with what they call the Calgary model, where you have just done something with this training of dogs and also by the owners or the opportunity to give the owner a command for training. So I think it would be really, really interesting if we could examine this in the Folketing, i.e. whether we could go more in that direction. I am glad that there are a number of parties that have expressed their wish either to support it here or to enter into a discussion on how we can look at changing this racial ban. I am sorry that some of the parties that were originally also the prime mover in introducing the racial ban have no more reflection that this has apparently not led to the desired effect and that very many people continue to suffer from it. So there is simply reason to review this, because the dog law as it now stands does not work as intended. The last thing I want to say is just this: it is so important that we move the focus to where it really should lie, so that we move the focus to how we can change the behaviour of irresponsible dog owners, so that we can all be more comfortable and avoid a few dog breeds being stigmatised in this way. So, as far back as the 1930s, I think it was, there was a discussion about whether the German Shepherd should be subject to a racial ban. Fortunately, it has not been, but it is a very good example of a German Shepherd, whom I mentioned earlier, may well be a dangerous dog, if it is poorly brought up I have experienced being bitten by German Shepherds myself when I was little but at the same time the German Shepherd can also be a wonderfully lovely and devoted dog if it is just properly raised. This shows very well that it is not the particular race that you have to deal with, but that it is the owners that we need to get hold of if we are to solve the problems. ParlaMint-DK_20160512202549	The debate is open. We start by giving the floor to the Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160512202610	Thank you for that, President. And thank you to the SF for having tabled the motion for a resolution. I believe that in Denmark we have a very sensible and clear distribution of responsibility for the management of the Nature Conservation Act and the Land Zone Regulations of the Planning Act between municipalities and states. The distribution means that, with a few exceptions, the municipalities, among others. the rules on the beach protection line and the clit protection line, lift the responsibility in the specific cases, that the state assists with guidance and advice, and that legal issues and disputes are resolved in the Nature and Environmental Complaints Board. It is a healthy system, also because it is the municipalities that have local knowledge in the specific cases. The proposers consider that there is a need to strengthen the monitoring of the enforcement of the Nature Conservation Act, the Planning Act and other laws on nature conservation as their purpose. Firstly, it wants the municipalities to have an obligation to report back to the Nature and Environment Complaints Board whether they have complied with the Council's decisions, and secondly, it wants annual monitoring reports to be prepared to be used to ensure an increase in counselling efforts towards the municipalities. The Government does not share the opinion of the proposers that there is a need for further strengthening of supervision and guidance efforts. Today, a great deal of guidance is being done by both the authorities and the complaints bodies on the interpretation of the legislation. This is done both by publishing actual guides on a topic and by providing guidance on the basis of concrete enquiries from the municipalities. I would like to make it clear that the government has great confidence that the municipalities administer according to the law and do their best to ensure that the decisions taken are correct. I also expect this, of course, in cases where a municipality has to re-examine a case as a result of a decision of the Nature and Environmental Complaints Board. According to the rules that apply today, there are many ways of intervening and making demands if that is needed in concrete terms. Firstly, the municipalities' decision under the Nature Conservation Act, the Land Zone Rules of the Planning Act and other laws can be appealed to the Nature and Environmental Complaints Board. Secondly, if it is considered that a municipality does not comply with the legislation applicable to them, for example, any possibility of bringing matters to the attention of the State administration is open. the Nature Conservation Act. Thirdly, citizens also have the opportunity to refer decisions to the Parliamentary Ombudsman after a case has been dealt with by the relevant appeal body or supervisory authority. The decisions of the Nature and Environmental Complaints Board, the opinions of the State Administration and the Ombudsman's opinions are all published and form the basis for future guidance efforts. In conclusion, I would like to point out that the legislation offers more scope for intervention if, in a specific situation, it is considered that there is a need to intervene. As a minister, as well as being responsible for the Land Zone Rules of the Planning Act, I can demand that a municipality provide relevant information and documents on matters dealt with by the municipality. It follows from the Municipal Government Act. I can also, in accordance with the rules of the Nature Conservation Act, require a municipality to provide information to be used to assess matters covered by the law. The government considers that the necessary instruments are available to ensure both guidance and supervision, and at the same time the government does not want to introduce a new and very comprehensive and resource-intensive supervisory system which, at the same time, imposes a comprehensive administrative task on the municipalities. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512210044	Thank you very much, and thank you to the SF for making this proposal to make a complaint back to the Nature and Environmental Complaints Board by enforcing municipal decisions and demanding an annual statement to the Resort Minister on the various decisions taken and their enforcement. I would like to say that when I sit and look at such a proposal, there are two issues that I think are important to discuss. One is for reasons of legal certainty: what exactly does it look like after the municipal reform, which Enhedslisten also addresses, in relation to the citizen's right towards the municipality? After all, the municipality is an authority and is much closer to the citizens, and it may therefore be more difficult to intervene in a number of cases, because one can be close to the person to whom one sits and has to be the authority, and one can be directly involved in some of these cases. A question arises as to whether we have screwed up our nature protection law in the right way. On the radical side, we would actually like to have the two major discussions rather than solve the niche problems, but solve them in a way that could turn into an extra bureaucratic layer if we do not see them in a wider perspective. In relation to the municipalities as an authority and the legal certainty of the citizens, it is clear that it is not right that 8%. of the cases are not being complied with. So if in a report we can write something about it and try to raise it to a slightly higher level and ask for some knowledge of how things really are going on in this whole area, so that we know how big the problem is, we will be very positive about it. The second issue that arises is to look at the way in which we protect our nature. Here I would like to say that there are much more important issues than this one. I think it is more important to discuss whether § 3 protection means that we will have more nature in Denmark or less nature in Denmark, and whether the legal certainty that exists for both the individual landowner and for the citizens of this country, because of legislation that can grow into and out of § 3, is the right way to protect nature. That is why we, from the radical side, have also wanted to raise the whole question of nature and have put forward a plan of nature, where, like us, we try to see it in the broad perspective: what do we really have to do to make one third of Denmark become nature? How are we gonna make it stick together? Is this reintroducing the green Danmark map? Can we get farmers on the wagon as natural carers by starting to restructure agricultural subsidies, just as they have done in England? We would like to have some vision for the port of the country, and we would like to have new clear rules for the coasts and beaches that still ensure that these open and free coasts are available, but that there is also a greater opportunity for local decision-making. So the two major discussions we would very much like to take part in with the SF, and it may be an offshoot of this proposal that we get closer to it. But right now, just putting in an extra bureaucratic layer and not really looking at the big issues that are, we are not prepared for.

ParlaMint-DK_20160512211608	Thank you for that, President. And I would like to thank the alternative for putting forward B 121. The government wants to protect the Danish nature and will give priority to efforts to halt the decline in biodiversity. The nature effort must be supported by professionally sound data and independent scientific advice. The government is very much in agreement with the alternative. However, there is already a comprehensive system of permanent, independent and professionally based monitoring, reporting and evaluations on nature and biodiversity. We already have a thorough knowledge of how our biodiversity is and what can be done to improve it. In the government, therefore, we do not think that 7 million should be set aside. kr. annual to another council in the field of nature. It doesn't make nature feel any better. It is already in the system how we collect data, evaluate and receive advice on biodiversity issues. We have, for example. a large monitoring programme, NOVANA monitoring, which is adjusted every six years. The programme provides data to the majority of the reports being made. This applies, among other things, to: the reporting to the EU under the Nature and Water Framework Directives. In the so-called country reports to the UN, we evaluate how the Danish nature effort contributes to the achievement of the goal of halting the decline of biodiversity. The country reports shall be sent to the Secretariat of the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity every four years. With the latest country report and a number of additional information, we have also contributed to the mid-term review of the EU biodiversity strategy from 2011. On the basis of contributions from all Member States, the Commission has prepared a report which was presented in October 2015. Research institutions at both Aarhus University and the University of Copenhagen regularly advise the Ministry of the Environment and Food and thus the government. This is scientific, independent advice. The qualified professional advice is a key element in the definition of new initiatives in the field of nature. A broad effort has already been made for nature, which will contribute to the objective of halting the decline in biodiversity. Here, the new plans for Natura 2000 sites are an important step towards improving the conditions of our endangered animals and plants, as well as the future river basin plans and the efforts of a new marine programme to improve our biodiversity. In addition, I expect the upcoming nature package to contribute to an additional effort to improve our biodiversity. Action is needed and the government would rather spend money on a better nature and biodiversity than creating another council in the field of nature. It is not knowledge of nature that is lacking, but action. The government is working on a solid professional basis of valid data and independent research-based advice. On this basis, the government ensures a balanced nature and environmental policy that contributes to the achievement of the UN and EU objectives of halting the decline in biodiversity by 2020. We are also looking at what initiatives should be included in a future nature package that can help to further enhance biodiversity. The Government cannot therefore support the Alternative's proposal to set up a Biodiversity Council.

Whether a biodiversity council can be the solution, I am not sure, because I think a better solution was a new government, but it may well be dragging on a little.
